A credit card is required, and remaining trial days are kept before the first charge. | Use this when the store is still on the free trial, since it requires a paid plan to remove the password |

Removing the password with a paid plan, step by step (older advice, 2021-22) [L0370.010]:

1. Open Online Store > Preferences in Shopify admin.
2. Click "Select a plan" to end the free trial, since the password cannot be disabled without a paid plan.
3. Choose a plan; the Basic Shopify plan is the recommended starting plan, at USD 29 per month when billed every 30 days.
4. Add a payment method (a credit card is required); the trial days still remaining are kept before the first charge.
5. Return to Online Store > Preferences.
6. Under Password protection, turn off "Enable password" and click Save.
7. Check the storefront in a separate browser or private window to confirm the "Opening Soon" password page is gone.

### Link and content checks

- Click-test every link on the site and confirm each one opens the correct destination with no 404 errors, in this order [L0167.004]:

1. Header menu links, including every item and every dropdown item.
2. Homepage collection sections, opening each collection.
3. Every other linked section or button on the homepage.
4. Footer links, especially the Quick Links and policy links.

- Click every link on the homepage, the collection pages, the about page and the policy pages. [L0855.179]
  1. Fix or remove any link that goes nowhere, is broken, or reloads the same page.
  2. Remove placeholder text and theme default labels, such as generic collection names, left over from installing the theme.
  3. Check that each product category matches what the store says it sells, and that the policy pages are rewritten for this business.
  4. Confirm that the contact method, shipping time and return policy are all filled in.

Note: L0855.179 is listed above as a single procedure with five steps; the numbering in the source is 1 to 5, with the click-test as step 1.

- Before going live, check for glaring issues such as broken links, product pages missing information, mispriced items and empty collections. The store should look professional from the first day. [L1446.022]
- Check the About Us page and the rest of the site for typos before turning on ads. [L1068.045]

### Critical elements: prices, buttons and add-to-cart

- A product page with the price and compare price accidentally hidden produced 40 add-to-carts and only 4 purchases for one student. Always verify that critical information is visible. [L0067.031]
- A missing or unclear add-to-cart button is a technical oversight that can silently kill conversions. Verify that all buttons and fields work before running ads. [L0067.032]
- A product listing that shows a price of $0.00 or a broken price looks like a bug and kills trust. Test the product page and cart before sending paid traffic. [L0657.039]
- Older advice (2019): check the store before turning on ads, in this order [L1068.045]:

1. Make sure the checkout works.
2. Make sure all links in the header and footer work.
3. Make sure every add-to-cart button works.
4. Check the About Us page and the rest of the site for typos.
5. Once confident, turn the ads on.

### Mobile and cross-device checks

- Check the store on mobile first, because most shoppers buy from phones and desktop views often look weaker. Make sure the layout looks trusting and polished on a phone. [L0657.037]
- Check the store on a phone as well as on desktop, because the mobile layout can look much better or worse than the desktop version. [L0656.022]
- Review a store on a phone in portrait view, not only on desktop, because desktop layouts can hide gaps where a product photo or GIF fails to fit. Count the taps needed to reach the real product page on the phone as well. [L0530.034]
- Check the mobile version separately from the desktop view at every stage of editing, because mobile is the main traffic source. Confirm that images, icon bars, reviews and FAQs look clean on mobile before publishing. [L1324.004, L1331.039]
- A product title shown vertically on mobile after Add to cart, while the cart shows it horizontally, looks broken and can cost a sale. A likely cause is a font size set too large for mobile. [L0837.005]
- Shopify checkout appears as a dropdown order summary on mobile but displays differently on desktop. [L0255.026]
- Scroll the whole page on mobile before linking the store from TikTok, to confirm that the lower half loads and that text and images display correctly. Most visitors arrive on their phone. [L1380.056]

Mobile-first store review, step by step [L0530.015]:

1. Open the store on a phone and count the clicks to reach the actual product page; a product page that takes around five clicks is a weakness.
2. Make sure the product page comes first on the page, with the product image, title, price and add to cart above everything else.
3. Move the product details, refund policy, and long text blocks below the main product information.
4. Place customer reviews above the email signup section and the footer.
5. Keep the footer last, and remove the "Powered by Shopify" credit only if your theme allows it.

Cross-device check before launch [L0552.054]:

1. Before launch, open the store page and every ad creative on a Windows PC and on a Mac.
2. Check the page on an iPhone and on an Android phone.
3. Fix any layout or visual differences before spending on ads, because a page can look correct on one device and broken on another.

- Older advice (2019): run a test purchase on desktop and on mobile to confirm orders go through, and check on mobile that all content stays visible and usable. [L0356.020]

### Test orders and checkout

- Place a test order before launch to check that the whole store works as intended. Reviewing it in the Shopify admin shows what a real customer order will look like. [L1302.001]
- Test order in Shopify [L1302.002]:

1. Open the store and go to the cart.
2. Remove most of the extra products, since a test does not require buying several units.
3. Go to checkout and enter any email address that uses the domain example.com.
4. Enter dummy shipping information.
5. Click Complete order.
6. Open the Shopify admin, go to Orders, and check that the test order appears.

- In Shopify Admin > Orders, a test order shows payment status payment pending, because it is not a live order and receives no payment. Its fulfillment status starts as unfulfilled. The list also shows the sales channel (Online Store), the order amount and the shipping method (here free shipping). [L1302.004]
- Before launching ad campaigns, place a full test order on your own store with your own credit card, going through the entire checkout. Check that every step works, then cancel the order, since the money returns to your account. [L1126.028]
- Test the payment flow on a new store [L1381.047]:

1. Place a real dummy order on your own store.
2. Cancel the order or issue the refund in Shopify.
3. Confirm the refund arrives quickly, which one seller reported as within seconds.

- Note on refund timing: L1126.028 says the money returns to the account after cancelling, while L1381.047 reports one seller's refund arriving within seconds. Both describe the same test; the refund timing depends on the payment provider and is not guaranteed by either source. [L1126.028, L1381.047]
- Older advice (2020): create a $1 test product on the store, add it to the cart, and go through a complete checkout, on both desktop and mobile. Then verify that the order confirmation appears and that the payment gateway processes without errors. This validates that checkout works before driving traffic. [L0268.019]

  1. Create $1 test product on store.
  2. Add to cart and go through complete checkout.
  3. Test on both desktop and mobile devices.
  4. Verify order confirmation appears.
  5. Confirm payment gateway processes without errors.

- When you create a test product to check the cart flow, turn off quantity tracking for it so you can add it to the cart from the storefront. This setting is only for testing and is not a recommended way to run a live product. [L1326.027]
- Test the checkout with a real sale before scaling ad spend, so you know orders can be completed end to end. [L0823.015]
- Run the test before expecting any orders, because a store that looks finished can still fail at checkout. [L1324.003]

### Compliance review

- Before launch, check the store against the pre-launch compliance checklist. A second reviewer can look at the same checklist on the live store to catch items that were missed. [L0970.166]
- Link all policy pages in the store footer before running any ads, for Google compliance. [L1446.030]
- Legal pages must exist before traffic: refund policy and shipping policy, with company name and email address. [L1345.073]
- Update the Legal section in Shopify Settings and make the Contact Us page list phone number, email and postal address. [L1145.051]
- Rewrite the policy pages for this business, not the theme defaults. [L0855.179]
- Link Privacy Policy, Terms of Service and Contact Us in the header and footer. [L1145.051]

### Viewing the store as a customer

- When you view your own store while logged in as admin, Shopify displays a bar stating that the store is not live and checkout is disabled. Use an incognito window or a different browser to see the customer view. [L0255.025]
- Shopify's bottom ribbon that reads Dropship appears only for admins logged into the Shopify admin while previewing the store. To confirm what customers see, open the storefront in an incognito window or while logged out of Shopify. [L0837.060]

### Launch criteria and checklists

Launch criteria, by source. The supplier and product numbers differ, so each applies to a different situation:

| Source | Criterion | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| L0356.026 (older, 2019) | Launch when the store works and has no obvious red flags; check that the cart and checkout work | General go/no-go |
| L0671.036, L0671.175 (older, 2021; merged, the two units differ only in "a few hours" vs "hours") | Launch when real products are uploaded, checkout works and the site is live. Google traffic starts once the product feed is approved, which can take hours or a few days. | Before Google traffic |
| L0808.009 (2026) | Launch once the first two suppliers are onboarded with live products uploaded and demo products removed. Launching before the full catalog is loaded is acceptable. | Minimum for launch |
| L0967.049 (older, 2020) | Before launching ad campaigns, products from at least two different suppliers must be uploaded. Ads can be turned on then, and new suppliers added over time. | Before ads |
| L0853.109 (older, 2021) | Upload at least two suppliers' products before the store is opened to traffic | Before traffic |
| L1446.030 | Confirm at least two suppliers are onboarded and uploaded; see the readiness check below | Before ads |
| L1446.008 | Eight supplier approvals and more than 500 uploaded products is more than enough to launch a store. Cross-sells are an advanced add-on not needed before the first launch. | Scale reference, not a minimum |
| L0963.158 (older, 2022) | Before expecting the first sale: the site is not behind a password, real products are uploaded, and Shopify Payments is set up | Before the first sale |
| L0834.130 (older, 2021) | Keep Shopify Payments turned off until real products are live, so nobody can place an order | Before real products are live |

- Payments timing: L0834.130 keeps Shopify Payments off until real products are live, and L0963.158 requires Shopify Payments to be set up before the first sale. Both apply in sequence: off while products are not yet live, then set up before the first sale. [L0834.130, L0963.158]
- Supplier count: the minimum is two suppliers with live products (L0808.009, L0967.049, L0853.109, L1446.030). The eight approvals and more than 500 products in L1446.008 describe a store that is more than enough, not a requirement. [L0808.009, L0967.049, L0853.109, L1446.030, L1446.008]
- Do not chase a perfect performance score before launch: a B grade on a performance tool is not a reason to delay launch, because large e-commerce brands often score lower than a typical new store. [L1446.019]
- A store that is set up is ready to launch with a high-converting design, because the store is where paid traffic lands and must turn visitors into buyers. [L0914.007]
- Publish before launch: see the readiness section, L0934.094 and L0934.165.

Launch readiness check, in order [L1446.030]:

1. Confirm that at least two suppliers are onboarded and their products are uploaded to the store.
2. Link all policy pages in the store footer before running any ads, for Google compliance.
3. Complete the conversion optimization module so the store is optimized for conversions.
4. Check for broken links, product pages with missing information, mispriced items, and empty collections.
5. Have a family member or friend who has not seen the store shop it while you watch. If no glaring problems remain, launch.

Family-member test, in order [L1446.023]:

1. Ask a family member or friend who has not seen the store to shop it as if buying one of your products.
2. Sit beside them while they browse and watch where they click.
3. Ask what stands out, what is not working, and what is confusing.
4. Thank them and make the changes they point out.

- Launch when the store works and has no obvious red flags. Have a friend or family member test whether they would buy from it, and check that the cart and checkout work. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0356.026]

Checklist before sending traffic [L1345.073]:

1. Payment processor: at least one payment method that accepts credit cards, plus PayPal for top markets.
2. Markets: all target countries are active in Settings > Markets, and a test checkout works in each.
3. Shipping prices: default rates are deleted, custom rates are set, and shipping is free or clearly priced.
4. Legal pages: refund policy and shipping policy exist, with company name and email address.
5. Checkout page: trust badges and payment icons are added, and taxes are disabled where no tax is charged.

Pre-launch Shopify store checklist, in order (Source D, 2026) [L1324.001]:

1. Create the Shopify store.
2. Install the Elixir theme.
3. Upload the logo.
4. Customize the homepage.
5. Check the mobile version of the store.
6. Import the product.
7. Add images and/or videos to the product page.
8. Add customer reviews to the product page.
9. Add FAQs to the product page.
10. Install the Kaching Bundles app.
11. Create the bundle offer.
12. Add quantity breaks to the bundle.
13. Highlight the best-selling offer.
14. Add the shipping, refund and privacy policies.
15. Complete a test checkout.
16. Review the store on both desktop and mobile.
17. Confirm the store is ready to receive paid traffic.

Launch checklist before the store is opened to traffic (older advice, 2021) [L0853.109]:

1. Remove the storefront password so the store is publicly live.
2. Upload at least two suppliers' products.
3. Configure all payment options.
4. Click every link and confirm nothing is broken.
5. Run a full test purchase through checkout to confirm it works.
6. Set up the ads.
7. Install analytics.

Full pre-launch checklist for a Google Shopping store (older advice, 2021-22 source). Use it as a reference; items about Google accounts apply only if you run those channels [L1145.051]:

1. Connect Shopify Payments and PayPal so checkout works.
2. Activate billing on the Google Workspace email.
3. Connect the public phone number to a phone you can answer, and test it with a call from another phone.
4. Set up a voicemail on the public number that lets callers request a callback.
5. Adjust the main navigation to the live products.
6. Show the phone number in the header and footer of the homepage.
7. Link Privacy Policy, Terms of Service and Contact Us in the header and footer.
8. Make the Contact Us page list phone number, email and postal address.
9. Update the Legal section in Shopify Settings.
10. Set at least one free shipping rate in Shopify shipping settings.
11. Set correct tax rates in Shopify settings.
12. Connect the domain to Shopify and set it as primary.
13. Turn off password protection.
14. Update the Preferences and General areas of Shopify.
15. Add online chat, review and email marketing apps.
16. Add USP information to product pages.
17. Open a Google Analytics account and connect it to Shopify.
18. Activate ecommerce tracking in Google Analytics.
19. Homepage: check that the USP highlight bars still match your offer.
20. Homepage: update the featured product and collection sections to show live products.
21. Products: delete all demo products.
22. Products: check that every product has the correct price.
23. Products: set correct stock levels, or mark products in stock with a placeholder quantity.
24. Products: add correct SKU, MPN and barcode where relevant.
25. Products: optimize product titles.
26. Products: check that each product image matches its product.
27. Products: set up and check the product page tabs.
28. Products: check that every product is in at least one collection and reachable from the main navigation.
29. Merchant Center: install the Feeds for Google Shopping app and submit the feed.
30. Merchant Center: make tax and shipping details match Shopify.
31. Merchant Center: update business information.
32. Merchant Center: verify the domain.
33. Merchant Center: verify the phone number.
34. Merchant Center: add branding.
35. Merchant Center: enable Free listings under Growth > Manage Programs.
36. Merchant Center: enable Shopping ads under Growth > Manage Programs.
37. Merchant Center: fix warnings under Products > Diagnostics.
38. Merchant Center: link the account to Google Ads.
39. Google Ads: set up the three-tier Google Shopping campaigns.
40. Google Ads: set up a trademark search text ad campaign.
41. Google Ads: create a payments profile and add billing details.
42. Google Ads: add the conversion tag through the Feeds for Google Shopping app.
43. Google Ads: add the remarketing tag through the Feeds for Google Shopping app.
44. Google Ads: link the account to Google Analytics.

Store setup order and the readiness items above are listed in the readiness section. Homepage and product items are also covered by the checks in the critical elements and link sections. [L1145.051]

- Old-label reference: the Pages banner in L1264.007 and the Themes banner in L1292.031 are the two places where the password notice appears; use either to find the Disable password button. [L1264.007, L1292.031]

### Traffic spikes and viral moments

- Before a large influencer post goes live, check that the store can handle a traffic spike. One brand saw more than 3,000 people shopping at once, compared with a normal load of 20 to 100 visitors. [L0790.014]
- A store that went viral was shut down by Shopify two to three days after the first viral sales, when order volumes were large. Customers reported they could not purchase, the seller noticed through comments, and the store was restored within about one hour, though more than $5,000 in sales was estimated to be lost during that hour. Shopify also requested paperwork such as proof of inventory once orders reached tens of thousands. [L0352.038]
- Check the comments on your videos regularly, because customers writing that they cannot buy is an early warning of a store shutdown. Shopify may also restrict accounts when order volume is very high. [L0352.039]
- A broken product link in a viral video or post sends traffic nowhere and directly reduces conversion. Check the link in every post before and during a viral moment. [L0655.005]
- Shipping or checkout issues on the store page can cost sales even when a video goes viral. Check the product page and checkout flow every time before promoting. [L0655.006]
- After adding the link to the bio, check it regularly to confirm the landing page and product page load without errors, since an error can appear after the link is applied. Fix any errors in Shopify when they show up. [L1380.040]

### Zero-sales and add-to-cart diagnostics

- Checking a product that gets add-to-carts but few sales [L0660.020]:

1. Check which payment methods are offered at checkout, including credit card and PayPal.
2. Check that the checkout works without errors.
3. Check the pricing point against the market.
4. Check the website speed.
5. If these are fine and sales are still low, the product is probably not good enough, so test a different product.

- Checkout test for a store with zero sales after add-to-cart [L0361.031]:

1. Check the shipping setup in Shopify and confirm that shipping rates are correct.
2. Check the market setup if you sell to several countries and confirm that each target country is active.
3. Try to check out with two random addresses in each target country, for example USA and Canada.
4. If checkout fails for those addresses, fix the setting that is blocking customers.

- Use this order when ad metrics are good but there are no sales, although add-to-cart and checkout ratios are healthy [L0774.007]:

1. Check the shipping options and whether shipping is being charged to the customer.
2. Check the markets: make sure every country you run ads to is enabled in Shopify Markets (Settings > Markets), because customers from a country that is not activated cannot choose it at checkout and abandon the cart.
3. Check that the payment providers work: create a 95% discount code for yourself and test a payment through PayPal, Shopify Payments or Stripe, whichever you use.
4. If a payment provider fails the test, uninstall it and install it again, which fixes setup problems in many cases.

- A common cause of zero sales with healthy funnel numbers is a targeted country that is not activated in Shopify Markets, so checkout cannot offer shipping to that customer. Check the country list against the countries in your ad targeting. [L0774.008]
- Review the drop-off rate at each step in analytics to find the bottleneck. Check in this order [L1225.040]:

1. Check the homepage.
2. Check the collection page.
3. Check the product page.
4. Check the cart.
5. Check checkout.

- A high bounce rate, for example 87 percent, is normal for ad traffic landing on product pages. Use Lucky Orange screen recordings to see why visitors leave and fix what causes it. [L0665.040]
- Diagnosis order when funnel numbers are weak: the offer, theme and checkout are the first suspects, as stated in the conversion section. [L1316.040]

### Post-launch maintenance

- Constantly update the store as you learn: change the header, footer and apps, and optimize the product page as more visitors arrive. A store needs ongoing maintenance rather than a single build. [L1444.043]
- Check the store on mobile at every stage of editing, since mobile remains the main traffic source after launch. [L1331.039]
- Keep checking the store links and product pages after launch, especially after changes to the bio link or the header and footer. [L1380.040, L0855.179]
- Re-check the store's speed score once the store is public. The score becomes available 1 to 3 days after protection is disabled, and a B grade is not a reason to pause sales. [L1292.020, L1446.019]
- A new Shopify store is password protected by default and shows a coming-soon page to visitors who are not logged in, asking for a password instead of showing the storefront. [L1294.001]

## Scaling the store: more suppliers, niches, content and delegation

### Adding suppliers and products in stages

**Supplier approval and catalogue build (older, 2020)**
1. Get approved with the first one or two brands. [L0468.072]
2. Upload every product from those brands, using CSV files for speed. [L0468.072]
3. Upload the next brands only after the first ones are complete. [L0468.072]
4. Aim for at least 100 different products before running Google ads. [L0468.072]

- A brand can start as a parked Shopify store with no products while the audience is being built. Products launch later, so audience reactions can guide what to sell. [L0487.025]
- The store can go live and advertising can start before every product is uploaded. In markets with many products, upload a first set, launch, and add more after launch (older, Source B 2021-22). [L1166.030]
- Keep adding quality supplier products while adjusting ads, even if the store is not yet profitable (older, 2022). [L0969.090]
- Source the product and confirm the supplier can ship it before running any ad. Never start an ad for a product you cannot fulfil. [L1171.033]
- Look for missing variants such as colours and sizes on competitor sites, for example light and dark wood finishes or new colours. Upload all available variants so shoppers who want a specific option choose your store. [L0894.011]
- Oberlo and AliExpress are named as sources for product supply and price markup. [L0838.004]
- Replace AliExpress or other Chinese products on an existing store with US suppliers only if the store's niche fits the niche criteria of modules one and two (2023). [L0964.094]
- A native Shopify supplier integration is a win. External enterprise-level apps are not recommended for a new store (2024). [L0971.038]
- Adding related products to an existing store is fine when analytics, Facebook pages, Google Merchant Center and Google Ads are already connected. Do not recreate them for a new store (older, 2021). [L0834.043]

**Product count: sources differ by purpose**

| Situation | Product count | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Testing a product on a general store | One product and one product page first; expand after sales show buyers | [L0388.011, L1217.037] |
| Multi-product store running Google ads | At least 100 different products (older, 2020) | [L0468.072] |
| Multi-product store target | At least 20 brands and 100 products; more variety drives more traffic and steadier sales than a one-product store (older, 2020) | [L0852.086] |
| Done-for-you base catalogue | Designer loads about 20 base products; owner adds more; stores usually need at least 100 products (older, 2022) | [L1281.017] |
| Overbuilt test store | Fashion store with more than 250 products and about USD 2,000 spent before demand was proven produced one sale and then a chargeback in the first month of ads | [L0660.045] |

- A store with no revenue has almost no resale value. Test a few products with a small budget before building a full catalogue. [L0660.045]

### Testing niches and products in one general store

**Start small and expand on proof**
1. Start a new product test with one product and a product page. [L0388.011]
2. Once sales show the audience buys, move to a collection page with several items to increase cross-sales and average order value. [L0388.011]
- A general store can be used for testing with one product page. Building a community and a brand requires a niche store, so move to a niche store once a product is proven. [L0335.052]
- Start with a general store under a brandable random domain rather than a single-product store, then test products one by one. Focus effort on landing pages with a strong offer and trust elements. [L0637.034]
- During product testing, a simple store is enough to collect data. Invest in a polished design, stronger branding and a product page builder after one product shows clear winning numbers. [L1176.046]
- A beginner is better off building one reusable general store personally than paying a freelancer to build a product page for every test product. Outsourcing each test becomes expensive, while skills carry over to every later test. [L1212.053]

**One domain, one general store for many tests**
- Run one brand-style, generic domain name (under 10 characters, easy to say and remember, for example made-up names checked with Namelix). Test products by adding a new product page rather than a new store. [L1410.028]
- The home page needs only 2-3 sections: a product banner, the product section, and trust badges such as priority shipping and a money-back guarantee. [L1410.028]
- Keep one general store domain for all products tested in the same broad category, such as shoes. Buy a new domain and brand only when a proven product moves to its own store or a different market. [L1131.031]
- Using one domain for all tests avoids the cost and friction of buying new stores and domains. A single-product focus with a strong product page still builds a brand identity. [L1217.039]
- In a new store, test one product at a time instead of loading dozens of products. Each product gets its own focused page. [L1217.037]
- Every new Shopify store requires its own home page, legal pages, notification settings and checkout setup. This is the main reason to keep testing products on one domain and only add a new product page. [L1410.046]
- Keeping a single store and swapping products is the cheaper option for early testing. Rebuilding under a new domain costs money and means losing the free trial. [L0372.024]
- To test many niches without paying for a new Shopify store each time, use one general store for all tests. When a winner appears, change the logo and name and keep the store setup, since opening a new Shopify store means paying again. [L0388.013]

**Product testing flow under one domain**
1. Build one generic homepage. [L1194.008]
2. Keep the About us, Contact, Track my order, FAQ and all policy pages the same across the store. [L1194.008]
3. Create one product page template and duplicate it for each product, so every tested product has its own URL (five products, five URLs). [L1194.008]
4. On the homepage, add a Shop now menu with a dropdown that lists every product. [L1194.008]
5. On the product pages, remove or hide the top navigation so the page reads like a single landing page. [L1194.008]
6. Launch one product in a single day. [L1410.029]
7. Check performance after 3 to 4 days. [L1410.029]
8. If it fails, create a new product page in about two hours and launch it within two hours. [L1410.029]
9. When a product wins, either turn the store into a niche store or devote a store to the winner, and keep testing new products under a separate domain. [L1410.029]

**Single-domain launch with unpublished backups**
1. Buy one brandable domain with no meaning, under 8-10 characters. Generate ideas with Namelix or ChatGPT, and combine two words into a new name if needed. [L1217.036]
2. Build one high-converting product page for the first product using a page builder such as PageFly or GemPages. [L1217.036]
3. Build the homepage with one banner that has the store name, a benefit-driven claim, and an image. [L1217.036]
4. Under the banner, add a short section about the store and why the product helps, then show the product. [L1217.036]
5. Prepare pages for the next products but keep them unpublished. [L1217.036, L1217.041]
6. After about three days, if the product does not work, publish the next product page under the same domain. [L1217.036]
- Keep unpublished product pages ready while the first product is tested. Judge a product after about three days of ad data before deciding whether to switch. [L1217.041]
- Judge timing differs between sources: about three days (above) or three to four days (the flow above). Use three to four days for a full read when running the step-by-step flow. [L1217.041, L1410.029]

**Converting a winner: options compared**

| Method | What happens | When it fits | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rename the store, keep setup and domain | Change the name to the niche, keep the domain, remove other products | The general store has one clear winner | [L0661.052] |
| Move the winner to its own store | Build a dedicated store and keep the general store running for further tests | The product needs its own brand or market | [L0372.021] |
| Convert to a single-product store, or add a new theme | Keep the existing Shopify store and pixel, so its sales data stays | Avoids losing sales data from the pixel | [L1212.022, L1212.023] |
| Add a second brand as a new theme | Add a theme inside the same store, upload the logo, switch the primary domain | You want a second brand without a new Shopify account | [L1131.030] |

**Winner-first store structure**
1. Start with a general store that carries several test products at once. [L0661.052]
2. Once one product clearly wins, rename the store to the niche, for example from caricia to caricia shoes. [L0661.052]
3. Keep the same domain name when renaming the store. [L0661.052]
4. Remove the other products and leave only the winning product line. [L0661.052]

**Store structure approach**
1. Start with a general store or a niche general store containing several products. [L1212.023]
2. Send ads to a collection page and let customers choose. [L1212.023]
3. Identify the best-selling product. [L1212.023]
4. Add a new theme focused on that product, optionally with a new domain and logo. [L1212.023]
5. Keep the existing Shopify store and pixel. [L1212.023]

- A general store can hold several products tested together. If a product wins, either convert the store into a single-product store or add a new theme focused on the winner, rather than building a new Shopify store, so the existing pixel keeps its sales data. [L1212.022]
- The usual setup is one product per store, or one general store used to test products and then move winners into their own store. Setting up a new store for a product can be quick, around three hours, if the design is reused. [L0372.020]
- For a fast setup for product testing, use these steps: [L1171.030]
  1. Use the Shrine theme, which has an inbuilt quantity discount. [L1171.030]
  2. Name the store with a general female first name rather than a product name, so the same store can test many products. [L1171.030]
  3. After sourcing a product, add it to the store and make the product page live. [L1171.030]
- When testing many products on TikTok, a store can be set up as a general deals-style store with a domain such as onlinedeals.com, instead of one store per product. Testing many products this way needs a lot of content. [L0561.016]
- Use a store that can sell a product line well. Build it around the product's season or niche, and expand with 5 to 10 new products per month to raise the store's product range. [L1440.018]

**Niche rules**
- Run each store around one niche, with multiple categories inside it. For example, a standing desk store can have categories for bamboo, whiteboard and L-shaped desks (older, 2021). [L0801.092]
- Keep unrelated niches in separate stores. Single-niche stores tend to have higher conversion rates, since the buyer experience is focused on one product type (older, 2021). [L0801.094]
- Use niche-specific stores rather than one broad store covering many categories. A niche store can be built even when competitors sell a wide range of categories (older, 2020). [L0664.040]
- A single store should cover one niche only (older, 2022). [L1281.017]
- Build a separate demo store for each new niche, even if you already run other stores. Mention your existing experience to suppliers and say the new store launches on a specific date (2023). [L0934.164]
- When products do not fit the theme of the first store, open a second store for them instead of forcing them in. The second store reportedly performed well within about three months (older, 2020 is not stated; source undated). [L0832.028]
- Do not create several identical clone stores with the same name, address and phone number to split a niche. This caused Google Merchant Center suspensions for one operator (older, 2021). Instead, after one sub-niche sells best in the main store, create a new store containing only those products. [L1090.047]
- To turn an existing low-ticket store into a high-ticket store, keep the same Shopify account, payment setup and categories when the niche is related. Replace the main products with more expensive items, update the logo if needed, and link a new domain in the Shopify admin (older, 2019). [L1002.079]

**Reusing a store when the niche is wrong**
1. Change the store name in the Shopify backend. [L1069.025]
2. Replace the logo. [L1069.025]
3. Replace all custom images that carry the old branding. [L1069.025]
4. Check that the colour scheme still fits the new niche. [L1069.025]
- The Shopify account can stay as it is, so this is easier than starting from scratch. [L1069.025]
- If the niche of a Shopify store turns out to be wrong, the store can be reused by swapping products and connecting it to a different domain name in the Shopify settings. This avoids building a new store from scratch (older, 2020). [L1111.186]
- An existing Shopify store can be completely rebuilt from scratch, including the domain name, theme, code and all products (older, 2022). [L1092.044]

### Additional stores and domains

**Costs of extra stores**
- Each separate Shopify store costs about $30. Run several stores inside one account with separate themes instead of opening new accounts. [L1131.032]
- Keep the old theme as a saved version. Seasonal stores, such as a Christmas store, can be republished later with the same logo and domain setup. [L1131.033]
- Old store versions remain stored as themes, so you can switch back and forth. [L1131.030, L1131.033]

**Several stores on one account (procedure)**
1. Create each new store with the same email address already used for the account. [L1428.024]
2. Go to shopify.com > Log in with email and click the green button "Create another store". [L1428.024]
3. All stores owned by that email then appear in a list in the account, for example on the left side. [L1428.024]

**Duplicating a store**
1. Create the new Shopify store with another email. [L1436.072]
2. In the old store, go to Online Store > Themes, download the theme and export it. [L1436.072]
3. Import the theme into the new store. [L1436.072]
4. Export the product and customer data and import it into the new store. [L1436.072]
5. Export the email and SMS lists (for example from Klaviyo or SMS Bump) and import them into the new store. [L1436.072]
6. Rebuild any settings that do not transfer, such as logo and notification settings. [L1436.072]

**Multiple countries and continents**

| Scenario | Recommended setup | Source |
|---|---|---|
| US and Canada (older, 2020) | One store serving both US and Canada | [L0268.073] |
| Other continents such as the UK (older, 2020) | Separate domains and stores for each continent | [L0268.073] |
| Canada and US, with separate marketing (older, 2021) | Two separate Shopify stores: domain.ca for Canada and domain.com for the US, with identical themes, logos and branding. This keeps marketing cleaner for ads, email and remarketing. Mention on About Us that you serve both countries. | [L0270.069] |

- The 2020 and 2021 sources differ. Use one store for North America if you do not need separate marketing per country. Use two stores if you want clean ad, email and remarketing campaigns per country. [L0268.073, L0270.069]

**Transfers**
- Transferring a Shopify store is complicated because the domain, the revenue source and account control all have to move cleanly. Many sellers use a broker or transfer service to handle it. [L1468.033]

### Copying competitor structures

**Finding and comparing competitors**
- Koala Inspector, a browser extension, shows which apps, themes, and upselling or cross-selling techniques competitor Shopify stores use. It also displays their best-selling products, revealing the sales tactics that work in your niche. [L0280.044]
- CopyFile is a French-language store analysis tool. It shows a store's theme, lets you export its code and images, tracks daily revenue and a 30-day forecast, shows best sellers and recent orders, and looks up advertising networks. The lesson reports its revenue numbers matched the presenter's own store. It costs 77 euros per month. [L0387.031]
- A collection URL with the best-selling sort order can reveal the best-selling products of any Shopify store. Append the path collections/all?sort_by=best-selling to the store domain. This works only on Shopify stores. [L0388.014]
- To compare a product across stores, search for the supplier's SKU first. If it does not show up, search by the product name and check whether the product exists on the other site. [L0894.022]
- To check whether two stores have the same owner: [L0656.023]
  1. Open the Contact Us page or the Terms of Service page of each store. [L0656.023]
  2. Note the email address shown on each page. [L0656.023]
  3. If the emails are identical, treat it as the final confirmation that the same people run both stores. [L0656.023]

**Documenting differences (2023)**
- When analysing competitor stores, document specific differences in writing: product descriptions (length, detail level, technical specs), features or benefits highlighted, warranty or guarantee mentions, shipping or return policies, pricing structure, page layout, customer testimonials, and social proof elements. This documentation guides what improvements to make on your own store. [L0251.057]

**Source D method: find, copy, adapt**
1. Find the product and list five to ten other stores that sell it. [L0600.003]
2. Open each store and pick the parts that look proven: offer, photos, FAQ, reviews, testimonials and page sections. [L0600.003]
3. Rebuild the offer on your store with the Coaching Bundles app from the App Store. [L0600.003]
4. Copy the photos, FAQ block, reviews and testimonials that fit your product. [L0600.003]
5. Copy the section layouts: video carousel, before-and-after block, timeline block and testimonial slider. [L0600.003]
6. Adapt everything to your own theme and add your own brand name to copied images. [L0600.003]
7. Launch the test store within about two days of finding the product. [L0600.003]
8. Once the test produces sales, customise the landing page so it looks unique. [L0600.003]

**Rules for copying**
- Copy only small parts from many competitor sites instead of large parts from one site. A store that mirrors a single competitor looks like a copy and loses credibility. [L0600.007]
- Many competitor stores start with a basic design and add more polished elements once daily sales are high. One observed store doing over $10,000 per day had added these elements after its early phase. [L0600.014]
- Copying the layout of a proven competitor store is a faster starting point than designing from scratch, and it lets you start testing sooner. A store selling the same product has already been shaped by what works in that niche. [L1249.001]
- Building a store from scratch takes a few hours, or almost half a day, for an experienced builder. Budget that time per store when planning pre-launch work. [L1249.002]
- Model your site on at least two to three competitors, borrowing their photos, text and layout structure. Then take the best elements from each and combine them into your own design, which is expected to outperform any single competitor. [L0897.005]
- Beginners in dropshipping often do not know what a winning store looks like, so studying proven stores makes building far easier. Modelling your store on existing successful stores avoids starting from zero. [L0897.004]

**Competitor research before the build (Source D 2026)**
1. Choose the product and confirm it through product research. [L0897.002]
2. Find three to five competitor stores that sell the same product and list them in the product research sheet. [L0897.002]
3. Take a screenshot of each competitor's homepage. [L0897.002]
4. Submit the competitor list and screenshots before starting to build your own site. [L0897.002]
5. If fewer than three competitors exist, tell the program team instead of stopping the process. [L0897.002]
6. Build the site only after this research is complete and submitted. [L0897.002]
- Competitor homepage screenshots must be made and shown before the store is built, not after launch. Some programs refuse to approve a site built without this prior competitor research. [L0897.003]
- Building a store from scratch without studying competitors often produces weak quality. Fixing a poor site after launch can take about a week of revision and is harder than getting it right the first time. [L0897.006]
- Building the site first and sending competitor research afterwards wastes time, because a poor build then needs a full week of revision. Competitor research should always come before the build starts. [L0897.007]
- The source range for competitors differs: five to ten stores to browse for proven parts (above), three to five to submit in the research sheet (above). Use five to ten as your browsing pool and three to five as the list you submit. [L0600.003, L0897.002]

**Store for a proven product (Source D 2026)**
1. Find a winning brand in your niche with long-running ads in the Meta Ad Library. [L1479.019]
2. Study its product offer and page structure as a reference. [L1479.019]
3. Use the ChatGPT prompt to rewrite its copy for your product and target avatar. [L1479.019]
4. Paste the rewritten text into your store. [L1479.019]
5. Add a few product images and short, clear texts, and keep the design clean. [L1479.019]
6. Add testimonials, an FAQ section and custom reviews. [L1479.019]

- A proven layout copied from another successful store is a far cheaper starting point than hiring a web developer for around $30,000 USD to build the whole store, which is not necessary for a store like this (older, source undated). [L1325.025]
- A store-build service can include design, uploading demo products and content pages, and setting up Google Analytics and Google webmaster tools. It does not include getting approved with suppliers, which the owner must still do (older, 2020). [L0852.115]

### Blog and content

**What counts as content and when to start**
- Content in this framework means anything on a site that is not a product page or an admin page, such as blog posts, infographics, YouTube videos, images, category or collection pages, eBooks, checklists and user-generated content. Product pages alone cannot fill the gap in the customer journey (older, Source B 2021-22). [L0414.020]
- Creating blog posts is optional and not required before supplier approval. [L0310.002]
- Blog content is not needed from day one. Add it only after paid traffic is performing well and profitable (older, 2021). [L1279.042]
- Blog content does not bring early sales. Do not copy other sites' articles. If you need content, have it written on freelance platforms such as Upwork or Fiverr, or buy PLR (private label rights) content (older, 2020). [L1277.057]
- In the first stage, focus on the store itself, conversion optimisation and paid ads. Content and blog posts come later, and a test site does not need blog posts (older, 2020). [L1277.058]
- Blog pages and blog posts should not be built or written during the initial site design. Blogging starts only after the business has launched, because it wastes time before launch (older, Source B 2021-22). [L1463.006]
- The blog can be a stated preference on the same domain: run it on the same website as the store, for example on WordPress with WooCommerce, so readers move between articles and products without leaving the domain. This is a stated preference rather than a proven requirement. [L0382.043]
- Use the native Shopify blog feature for content. SEO results depend on the theme, post optimisation and plugin speed, not on the platform (older, 2021). [L0962.034]

**Blog structure and admin**
- Create one blog per store. Shopify allows multiple blogs, but the standard practice is to use a single blog per store. [L0310.003]
- Blog post creation in Shopify:

  **Navigation:** Admin Panel > Online Store > Blog Posts

  **To create a post:** Click "Create Blog Post"

  **Fields:**
  - **Title:** the blog post headline (example: "Top Stand-Up Paddle Boards, Top Surfboards for 2024")
  - **Document editor:** area to insert text, images, and videos
  - **Excerpt:** summary text displayed on the homepage or main blog directory
  - **Featured Image:** image displayed at the top of the blog post
  - **Author name:** writer's name; can be ghostwritten
  - **Blog:** selection of which blog the post belongs to
  - **Blog post template:** use the default template (do not change)
  - **Comments:** toggle to enable or disable reader comments
  - **Search Engine Preview:** editable SEO metadata

  [L0310.004]
- Shopify Magic is a built-in AI tool marked by a star icon in the blog editor. Use it to rephrase, extend, or simplify blog post content. [L0310.005]
- Shopify automatically creates one default blog category named 'news' when the store is set up. All blog posts go to this category by default unless additional blog categories are created. [L0312.010]
- To find and change a blog post's category, open the blog post in your admin panel, scroll to the Organization section, and select or change the blog category. Blog categories are created by clicking 'create a new blog' in the Organization section. [L0312.011]
- To change the blog author name: go to Online Store > Blog posts, open the post, then in the right sidebar under Organization change the Author field (2023). [L0855.094]
- Hidden blog posts do not appear in the blog listing of the theme. Each post must be set to visible before it shows up in the listing. [L1265.007]
- Create an editorial in Shopify under Online Store > Blog posts by adding a new article in the blog, or under Online Store > Pages for a standalone page. Page builder apps such as PageFly or GemPages can be used to design the layout. [L0578.027]
- Shopify supports both the blog and the pages function for topical authority articles, so either can be used for that content. Collection pages are created as separate category pages in the store. [L1389.023]
- Shopify has a built-in blog function, so holiday blog posts can be created directly in the Shopify admin without extra apps. [L1224.035]
- Blogs are built into Shopify. Create one from the Shopify admin by clicking create blog and then adding posts. This is the simplest place to publish SEO content for seasonal promotions (2025). [L1287.032]

**Help and AI tools for content work**
- Use Shopify live chat for platform-specific setup questions instead of guessing. Ask exactly what you want to do so the answer is correct the first time (older, 2022). [L0963.047]
- For Shopify setup steps, follow the official Shopify help documentation because it is kept up to date. The admin AI assistant is trained on Shopify's own help center and can troubleshoot a step you are stuck on (2024). [L1285.038]
- Never let an AI tool write directly into the live Shopify store. Do the QA outside Shopify first, and only then apply the changes in bulk. [L1269.041]
- When using custom rules with Shopify and a Claude MCP connection, set the rules for internal links explicitly, then have the connection apply only what those rules allow. Keep a manual review step before the rules are executed. [L1269.042]

### Seasonal setup

**Holiday and seasonal stores**
1. Have a store template and product catalogue ready before Q4 begins. [L0309.037]
2. Add multiple product categories (Black Friday, Christmas, Valentine's Day, evergreen) to one store. [L0309.037]
3. Run different ad campaigns and creative variations per product, but fulfil all orders through the same store to centralise inventory and customer data. [L0309.037]
- Create a seasonal section and header message before a holiday, such as a Mother's Day gifts section with a note that gifts arrive in time for May 13. Make it visible early enough for buyers to order and receive the gift before the date. [L0355.009]
- A holiday gift store should look built for the occasion. Use a brand name, logo, colours and wording tied to the holiday, and list only a few products. Shoppers who worry about choosing the wrong gift buy more confidently in a store that looks designed for that purpose. [L1214.016]
- Keep old store versions such as a seasonal Christmas store as saved themes, and republish them later with the same logo and domain setup. [L1131.033]

### Store build or outsourcing

**Two paths and what each delivers**
- A store can be built in two ways: do it yourself by installing the provided theme and modifying it, or outsource the design to a specialised dropshipping web design team. Both paths lead to a store ready for supplier approval. [L1061.003]
- You can build your demo store yourself by completing all Module 3 lessons, or outsource the entire build to the Source A Done-For-You team to receive a finished, supplier-ready store. Either way, you retain 100% ownership. [L0069.005]
- Outsourcing store design is an acceptable choice, while the owner keeps control of supplier and traffic decisions. Many owners build their first store themselves and outsource design once the business earns money (older, 2019). [L0467.043]
- Outsourcing the Shopify store build to a paid setup service is optional and costs extra on top of any training or programme fee. The seller can always build the store personally instead (older, Source B 2021-22). [L1466.001]
- A done-for-you store build means a team builds a store from a proven design after checking the niche. Build quality is guaranteed, but revenue or a fixed return is not. An established profitable store sells at a multiple of its monthly net profit rather than at the build price. [L0941.103]
- A custom dropshipping store built by an outsourced web design team is typically delivered in about five business days, so the builder does not have to go through the store build steps themselves. [L1061.004]

**Timing and lead times**

| Path | Lead time | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Build yourself, experienced builder | Under five hours, skill grows with each store | [L1380.053] |
| Build yourself, a few hours to almost half a day | Budget per store in pre-launch planning | [L1249.002] |
| Build yourself with a reused design | About three hours | [L0372.020] |
| Template-based store (tested template, only logo and images changed) | About 3 to 5 hours; about a full day including creatives, with two people | [L1215.029] |
| In-house design team, team not busy (older, 2022) | About one week; submit early because timing depends on workload | [L0803.085] |
| In-house design team, queue (2023) | First come, first served by when the store information form is submitted, not when purchased. Two to three weeks in January 2023; plan about three weeks | [L0804.041] |
| Done-for-you, volume of 2020 (older) | About five business days from the day information is submitted | [L0967.045] |
| Done-for-you, 2022 (older) | 3 business days to 2 weeks, depending on team workload; confirm the current ETA with support | [L0854.072] |
| Done-for-you, June 2024 | About five to seven business days (lead time changes over time) | [L0856.065] |
| Done-for-you, 2024 | Typically about 3 to 5 business days, though the backlog can change it | [L1115.061] |
| Done-for-you, 2026 | Often two to three days when the design team has availability | [L0808.023] |
| External developer | About 10 to 14 days; design delivered first in Figma, live Shopify store about one to two weeks later | [L1215.030] |

**Costs of outsourcing**

| Option | Cost | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Complete store build from scratch, including product setup, design and basic optimisation (older, 2019) | $1,497 USD | [L0243.020] |
| External developer | About $700 USD | [L1215.030] |
| Web developer for the whole store | Around $30,000 USD; not necessary for a store like this | [L1325.025] |
| Failed fashion build (more than 250 products) | About USD 2,000 spent before demand was proven; one sale, then a chargeback in the first month of ads | [L0660.045] |

- Shopify store build done-for-you is a paid service (June 2024). [L0856.065]
- Confirm whether the logo is included before ordering. A done-for-you store build includes logo design, so the buyer does not need to supply a logo, but confirm this is included in the specific package (2024). [L0806.103]

**What a done-for-you design changes (2026)**
- A done-for-you design swaps the theme only. Existing products, brands, pages and customisations are kept, and policy pages are updated to meet Google compliance. [L0808.021]
- Done-for-you store packages can differ. Some do not include Google account setup, so check what a package includes before buying. [L0808.022]
- When a done-for-you design service rebuilds an existing Shopify store, the designer works through a collaborator code in draft mode. The current store is not deleted and no new Shopify account is created. Existing products, brands, pages and customisations carry over, and only the theme and policy pages change. [L0808.053]

**Ordering a done-for-you build (2020, older)**
1. Enrol in the web design programme. [L0967.046]
2. Receive the intake email and complete it with the Shopify store login, store name, niche, links to sites you like, and what you like about them. [L0967.046]
3. The design team builds the store and uploads demo products. [L0967.046]
- Web design is ordered from the members area sidebar, through the option labelled "let my team build you a beautiful e-commerce website". This starts the intake process for the store build. [L0967.105]
- Before ordering a web design build, completing the first two training modules is enough. The remaining modules do not need to be finished before the store is built. [L0967.103]
- For the first store, do not build two stores at once. Start with one store, because mistakes made with the blueprint steps can only be fixed once. [L0967.051]
- Learn Shopify while doing a task (older, 2020):
  1. Open the training video on one side of the screen and the Shopify admin, logged in, on the other side. [L0967.047]
  2. Watch about 30 to 60 seconds of the video, then pause it. [L0967.047]
  3. Repeat each click in Shopify while the video is paused. [L0967.047]
  4. Continue segment by segment until the task is done. [L0967.047]

**Starting a done-for-you design after purchase (2022, older)**
1. Open the order confirmation email sent after buying. [L1281.126]
2. Click the link in it and fill in the web design questionnaire with details about the new store. [L1281.126]
3. If the email cannot be found, search the inbox for 'web design questionnaire'. [L1281.126]
4. If it is still missing, email support and request the form. [L1281.126]
5. The build starts once the questionnaire is submitted, at the time the buyer is ready. [L1281.126]

**Queue and timing rules**
- Web design orders are queued first come, first served, based on when the client submits the store information form, not when they purchase. (2023) [L0804.041]
- A store cannot be rebuilt for a different niche once the web design has been built from scratch. A new niche would require a second store, which is effectively a second job. Small tweaks are still possible (2023). [L0804.042]
- Delay the store build until content shows some traction, such as a few thousand views, since a store built before any demand can waste about 10 hours. Build the store in parallel with content only if you have no web-building experience. [L1380.033]
- A beginner's full build: learn first, then build one reusable store personally (procedure):
  1. Build the store yourself rather than paying a freelancer for each product. [L1212.037]
  2. Use the free course section about building a store with the Refresh team. [L1212.037]
  3. Watch the video on building a branded product page with PageFly and implement it at the same time. [L1212.037]
  4. Use a free website template from the course (Website Building channel, Paper Loop template). [L1212.037]
  5. Create a landing page with PageFly, which is free. [L1212.037]
  6. Run ads to the store. [L1212.037]

**Build yourself first, then delegate**
- Learn to build a Shopify store yourself first, including theme, colour scheme, branding, copywriting and apps, so you know which design gets the best results. Only then delegate the build, giving the person your exact structure. [L1358.008, L1360.009]
- Learning Shopify store building is a lasting skill, not only for your current dropshipping business. Owning it lets you judge a store build and decide what to change in the theme, branding and copy. [L1358.026]
- A delegated store build can save a day or two of work. That time can be used to prepare creatives and set up Facebook or TikTok ad campaigns. [L1358.009, L1360.010]
- A delegated store setup can include the theme, logos and branding, the copy, the apps you specify, and the payment providers, so the store is ready to run ads. [L1358.010]
- The best case is a team member who already has a product they want to test. Give them your store structure, the apps to install and the branding requirements so they can set the store up for ads. [L1358.027, L1360.010]
- Delegation steps:
  1. Build the store yourself at least once to learn which theme, design, branding and apps work for you. [L1360.011]
  2. Give the team member a product that you want to test. [L1360.011]
  3. Tell them to build the store following your structure. [L1360.011]
  4. Have them create the logos and branding, following your guidelines. [L1360.011]
  5. Tell them which apps to install and how to configure them, especially Klaviyo and SMS Bump. [L1360.011]
  6. Once trust is established, have them install the payment providers so the store is up and running. [L1360.011]

**Freelancers and task services**
- Hiring developers or designers for store builds can be done through freelancers who specialise in Shopify. Expect the developer's skill to matter more than their theme knowledge, since HTML skills transfer across themes and platforms (older, 2021). [L0801.096]
- The course's store design and VA sourcing is done via Upwork or Taskrabbit, with Source A support for small jobs. No internal VA list is provided (older, 2022). [L0969.093]
- Every few months, have a freelancer audit the store for broken pages, page speed problems and oversized images that should be compressed. Use this as a project-based task rather than a permanent staff position. [L0921.011]
- For most Shopify store tasks, spend about 30 minutes using Shopify support documentation and search before paying for help. Hire a Shopify developer only for custom coding beyond that (2024). [L1115.060]

**Outsourced setup, buyer side (Source B 2021-22, older)**
1. Open the setup service page and click the button to request the service. [L1466.002]
2. Fill in the request form. [L1466.002]
3. The provider contacts the buyer and states what it needs. [L1466.002]
4. The buyer supplies the branding: brand colours, colour schemes and the business name. [L1466.002]
5. The provider logs into the buyer's Shopify account and configures the store until it is ready to call suppliers. [L1466.002]

**Page-building tools**
- Custom fields combined with drag-and-drop layouts let team members build pages without hand-coding HTML. This reduces errors such as unclosed div tags and speeds up onboarding of new staff (older, Source B 2021-22). [L0949.017]
- Learning Shopify and running the store in-house: a template-based approach uses one tested template for every store, changing only the logo and product images. [L1215.029]

### B2B and trade pages

**Why a trade page**
- For business-to-business buyers such as interior designers, a separate trade page with its own contact form and a list of trade buyers' logos builds trust and repeat orders. Logos can be added as the list of past trade customers grows (2024). [L0360.022]
- High-ticket industrial products can be offered to business buyers on a separate page or collection named To The Trade. The page should describe business-to-business sales, list the industrial products, and include a contact form so buyers can request quotes for large orders before buying (2022). [L0963.156]

**Adding a trade page (2022, older)**
1. Create a page in Shopify named to the trade. [L0429.036]
2. State that bulk orders and trade discounts are available. [L0429.036]
3. Add a contact form where buyers can request a custom quote. [L0429.036]
- No other change to the marketing approach is needed. [L0429.036]

**Adding a trade page (2025)**
1. Create a page that duplicates the contact page, including the contact form. [L0972.023]
2. Replace the top heading with To the Trade and add trade copy describing bulk discounts, custom quotes and expedited shipping. [L0972.023]
3. Link the page in the footer menu, and sometimes in the header menu. [L0972.023]
4. Once business orders arrive, add a list of businesses you have sold to, to show credibility. [L0972.023]

**Trade page text template (2025)**

```
To the Trade

We work with [interior designers / businesses / contractors] nationwide for all of their [client / project / office] needs. We offer expedited shipping, bulk discounts and custom quotes.

For a business order, call us at [phone number], email us at [email address], or fill out the form below with your name, email address, phone number, the items you are interested in, the quantity, and the date you need them by. We will reach out to you right away.

Brands we have worked with: [logo list of business customers you have sold to]
```

[L0972.024]

**Contact form and wholesale accounts (older, 2020)**
- On a "To the Trade" page, use a contact form instead of a separate order form, with these fields: name, email, phone number, business name, product of interest and number of units. Mention that bulk or quantity discounts are available on the page. [L0961.013]
- Create B2B wholesale accounts with a Shopify app that assigns account-level pricing. Logged-in trade customers then see lower prices and can add discounted items to their cart. [L1088.030]

### Learning resources and help sources

**Free learning resources and reference tools**
- Free learning resources for web development include the YouTube channels Web Dev Simplified and Fireship, plus free online programming courses from Google. Books are also recommended for learners who prefer reading. [L0709.047]
- Stack Overflow is useful for finding fixes to specific technical problems, though its answers are very technical for beginners. Reddit can be used to ask which developers people recommend, but beginners should expect blunt replies. [L0709.048]
- The Shopify Help Center at help.shopify.com documents adding products, product details, variants, product media, collections and importing and exporting. Use it to check small questions about fields or variants. [L1166.002]
- Build an operations resource list for each team member by collecting the Shopify Help Center guides for these tasks: creating collections, creating orders, and creating discounts. Add the links to the corporate board so staff can learn on their own (older, Source B 2021-22). [L1396.007]

**Task-based services for small jobs**
- Task Husky is a marketplace for small, one-off site jobs such as installing a widget on a product page. Prices start from about US$39 per task, which is far cheaper than hiring an agency for a full site relaunch (older, Source B 2021-22). [L0719.031]
- Freelance marketplaces such as Upwork often focus on full site relaunches, so they can be a poor fit for minor updates. Use task-based services for small refreshes and check the price before committing (older, Source B 2021-22). [L0719.032]

#### Further details

- A one-product store can be converted to a multi-product store by renaming it and changing the linked URL. Keep the existing Shopify account and theme, since this avoids paying for a new trial. [L0852.085]

## Other details

### Build order: niche and modules first

- Choose your first niche before you build any store, because the store's design and content depend on it. A store build started without a selected niche is out of sequence. [L1061.001]
- Complete Modules 1 and 2 before you start Module 3 store building, and follow the lessons in sequence for best results. [L0069.003]
- Module 3 is the store-building checklist:
  1. Start with lesson 3.0.
  2. Complete the action task in each lesson.
  3. Click Complete when done. You keep access to reopen lessons. [L0276.043]
  4. Progress in order through 3.1, 3.2, 3.3 and beyond. [L0276.043]
  5. Complete all lessons so that every required store component is built. [L0276.043]
- A beginner without web design experience can build a first dropshipping store by following a step-by-step process, completing the steps in order. [L1061.002]

### Shopify build sequence

**Standard build (merged from two identical sequences):**

1. Sign up for Shopify and start the store-building training.
2. Keep building the store, asking for help when you get stuck.
3. Finish a demo store.
4. Remove the storefront password so the store is publicly reachable. [L0393.005, L0532.006]

**Build with supplier approval:**

1. Sign up for Shopify.
2. Set up the theme, using tutorials and repeating steps until it works.
3. Build collections and navigation to group products logically.
4. Set up the demo store and take it live.
5. Call the first supplier and get approved.
6. Upload all products to the store. [L1437.012]

**Beginner build with branding:**

1. Choose the Shopify theme and set up the store.
2. Set up branding, such as a logo, from a freelancer platform such as Fiverr.
3. Add the niche products and product data once supplier approval comes in.
4. Clean up the design and make product pages tidy.
5. Launch the store and set the traffic funnel to start selling. [L0458.034] (older: 2021-22)

**Split-screen method:**

- Follow the training step by step on one side of the screen and do the same action in the Shopify admin on the other. A complete store can be set up in a few evenings or over a weekend. [L1111.064] (older: 2020)

### Build time: what each source says

The sources do not agree on build time. Each figure applies to a different scope and situation.

| Source | Time | Applies to |
|---|---|---|
| Source D 2026 | About 15 minutes | Pump-and-dump store with product name, product photos, product videos and an FAQ section [L0347.023] |
| Source C | About 30 minutes | Simple organic store that copies a proven store structure and adds reviews, upsells and bundles [L0352.012] |
| Source C | About 1 hour, plus about 1 more hour for a page builder landing page | Beginner store build tutorial and page builder landing page tutorial [L1017.003] |
| Source C | About 1 day when starting out; a few hours once experienced | Reported as about 3 hours for an experienced builder and 4 to 5 hours for an earlier build [L1444.039] |
| Source A (older: 2020) | About 21 days | Someone working 8 hours a day with a step-by-step launch plan [L0468.074] |
| Source A (older: 2020) | A few evenings or a weekend | Split-screen build method [L1111.064] |

- The sources do not reconcile the 21-day figure with the two-week limit. The 21-day figure describes a full-time beginner (8 hours a day) following a step-by-step launch plan. [L0468.074]
- The two-week figure is the limit for the whole path from product selection to live. [L1017.003]
- Ongoing work after launch takes about 30 minutes to 2 hours per day. [L0468.074] (older: 2020)

### Design priorities for a new store

- Store appearance is secondary to choosing the right product and the right audience. A plainly designed store selling the right product to the right people will generate sales, while a beautiful store selling the wrong product will fail. [L0280.011]
- Do not spend time perfecting navigation menus, link placement or visual design at the early stage. Focus first on the product page and the homepage. [L0280.012]
- A store does not need to look perfect to produce early results. Focus first on three elements: a strong offer, a strong ad and good product images. [L1172.046]
- A weak-looking store can still sell, but a well-built Shopify store with a clean design and a product page builder earns more customer trust than competitors with poor sites. [L1176.032]
- Do not judge a store by your own buying habits. Many customers buy from simple stores when the offer is clear and attractive. [L1176.033]
- Building the store can wait until the account reaches about 1,000 followers or starts going viral, if your store-building skills are weak. If you build quickly, you can set it up earlier. [L0820.040]
- Spending months perfecting a custom store before launch wastes time and money. Launch a simple, high-converting store quickly, then focus on the product, customer service, logistics and marketing. [L1251.029]
- Beginners often lose days on small details such as button colour, the add-to-cart design or whether the supplier can print a logo on the product. These matter far less than getting the product, the ads and the store live quickly. [L1454.023]
- A beginner does not need professional design skills to build a clean, converting e-commerce site. The goal is a site that looks clean and professional, that suppliers can review, and that is good enough to launch and make first sales. Perfection delays launch, so a simple site is preferred over a perfect one. Advanced functionality and professional designers can be added as the business grows. [L1463.001] (older: 2021-22)
- Build only what is needed for launch and postpone everything else. Time spent on non-essential pages before launch is wasted, which includes blogging and any design work that does not help the first sales. [L1463.015] (older: 2021-22)

**Case: a simple store became a product**

- Starting point: the operator spent a lot of time building perfect stores across several dropshipping projects, which led to the insight that a simple, quick-to-launch store converts best. [L1251.046]
- Action: that insight became Debutify, a plug-and-play theme and software business built around fast store launches. [L1251.046]
- Result: the software took about 12 months to get off the ground, with significant initial investment and then sustained marketing before it became a working product. [L1251.046]

### Conversion-ready store (Phase 3)

- Phase 3 store building (Days 31 to 45) must establish trust within three seconds of a customer viewing the landing page, because trust is the primary conversion barrier for a new brand. [L0199.005]
- A store built for conversion in Phase 3 must include:
  1. An Elixir or Shrine theme.
  2. A clean product page design.
  3. Strong offers, such as bundles, buy-one-get-one or free packs.
  4. Customer reviews, trust badges and a satisfaction guarantee.
  5. AI-generated product photos.
  6. User-generated content (UGC) placed above the fold. [L0199.006]
- Evaluate store pages for:
  1. High-quality product imagery.
  2. Clear product descriptions.
  3. Customization and variant options.
  4. A professional layout. [L0209.037]
- Landing pages for problem-solving products should show the use case and the effect of the product visually, for example a short GIF of the relief shown on the body. A visual demonstration explains the benefit faster than text. [L0871.021]
- Trust claims should be specific. In a weak store, replace vague trust claims with named partners, logos and verifiable details. [L0355.022]

### Using competitor stores as benchmarks

- Do not use cheap-looking stores, often built by Chinese suppliers trying to compete directly, as design models. Their pages tend to look like scams and damage trust in your own store. [L1249.012]
- A store running a shared commercial theme or template, such as Atlas, is a sign that it is a dropshipping store and not a brand-built site. Recognizing this helps you separate dropshipping competitors from professional benchmarks. [L1249.013]
- Use only competitor stores that look clean and load at normal speed as design benchmarks. Skip slow or broken sites. [L1249.014]
- Judging whether a store looks good is a visual check any online shopper can make. A professional product page is recognizable without special expertise, so use your first impression as a quick filter. [L1249.015]
- To see which products a Shopify store sells best, add /collections/all?sort_by=best_selling to the store's domain. This lists its products by sales rank and gives a free way to read a competitor's top sellers before you decide what to test. [L1184.063]
- A pump-and-dump store does not need a full brand build. A simple store with the product name, product photos, product videos and an FAQ section is enough. [L0347.023]

### Fixing a weak store with limited time

When you take over a weak store with limited time, set priorities in this order:

1. Remove every product that carries a trademark or licensed character you do not have the rights to sell.
2. Split the navigation and homepage into categories by the recipient or buyer group the store serves.
3. Rewrite the header so the empty space carries a short buyer-facing message, such as a free add-on or a seasonal date.
4. Delete empty default Shopify sections, such as an unused video block or an empty promotions box.
5. Replace vague trust claims with named partners, logos and verifiable details. [L0355.022]

Note: the navigation rule for a new store (focus on the product page and homepage first) and the navigation step above (splitting categories in a weak store) apply to different situations. [L0280.012, L0355.022]

### Store settings and theme details

- Shopify Settings > Custom data is an advanced section for metafields. A new store does not need changes here and can be ignored at launch. [L1305.046]
- Store-level settings, apps and other logistics are set up in separate steps after the design and trust pages. Do not spend time on them while building the product page itself. [L1331.040]
- Dynamic placement applies per screen size. A minimum breakpoint of 999 with the maximum set to None targets screens from that width upward. Medium screens need their own breakpoint range, selector and placement location. [L0557.023]

### Trust documents, copying and legal protection

- Create unique PDF documents, such as warranty terms, installation instructions and product specifications, and place your logo on each one. The logo helps protect them from being copied and supports later takedown claims. [L1357.028]
- Copy-protection code on a store can be bypassed by determined users, so do not rely on it to protect your content. [L0968.045] (older: 2021)
- If a competitor copies your store, submit the copying site's links on Shopify's DMCA page. If the copy is not changed, it is typically taken offline within about 24 hours. [L0855.102]
- If a supplier asks whether your store is EDI compliant, explain that orders are sent as automatic purchase orders by email. You do not need to claim an EDI setup. [L1277.062] (older: 2020)
- When you want to discuss a specific part of your store, include a link to that section of the website in your pre-call email. This lets the reviewer look at it before the call. [L0364.010] (older: 2021-22)

### Split testing on Shopify

- On-page split testing is harder on Shopify than on platforms like WordPress. Two practical routes exist: a dedicated split-testing tool, or an app that manages the test and collects the data. [L1364.034] (older: 2021-22)

**Page split test with Google Optimize (as described in the source):**

1. Duplicate the original product page so both versions are identical.
2. Give the duplicate its own URL.
3. Set the duplicate to noindex, using a noindex app, to avoid duplicate content problems.
4. Create the test in Google Optimize so it redirects a share of traffic to the duplicate while the rest stays on the original. [L1364.035] (older: 2021-22)

### Digital product stores

- Shopify is recommended as the hosting platform for a digital product store. A Shopify app installed from the app store handles automatic digital delivery by emailing the product to the buyer right after purchase. [L0513.023]
